People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s not terrible, exactly, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in a dramatic way, no. It’s more that I just can’t seem to feel finished with it until I’ve checked again. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know perfectly well I already looked and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been having trouble just… moving on from checking things. Boxes, lists, drawers in the condo—that sort of thing. I’ll look, and I know I’ve already looked, but I still find myself going back over them again before I can leave it alone. My wife is usually waiting for me, and I know it’s frustrating for her. It’s not terrible, exactly, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in a dramatic way, no. It’s more that I just can’t seem to feel finished with it until I’ve checked again. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know perfectly well I already looked and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been having trouble just… moving on from checking things. Boxes, lists, drawers in the condo—that sort of thing. I’ll look, and I know I’ve already looked, but I still find myself going back over them again before I can leave it alone. My wife is usually waiting for me, and I know it’s frustrating for her. It’s not terrible, exactly, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in a dramatic way, no. It’s more that I just can’t seem to feel finished with it until I’ve checked again. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be symmetrical or exactly right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of things having to look even or line up exactly. It’s more… I’m checking whether I missed something, I suppose. If a box is a little untidy, that doesn’t bother me much by itself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been having trouble just… moving on from checking things. Boxes, lists, drawers in the condo—that sort of thing. I’ll look, and I know I’ve already looked, but I still find myself going back over them again before I can leave it alone. My wife is usually waiting for me, and I know it’s frustrating for her. It’s not terrible, exactly, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in a dramatic way, no. It’s more that I just can’t seem to feel finished with it until I’ve checked again. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be symmetrical or exactly right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of things having to look even or line up exactly. It’s more… I’m checking whether I missed something, I suppose. If a box is a little untidy, that doesn’t bother me much by itself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking to make sure everything is under control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair… though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s like I need to feel that I haven’t let something slip by before I can move on. So I’ll check the same boxes, or the lists, or those drawers at the condo again, even though I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting, and I know she’d probably like me to just leave it be, but I have trouble doing that until it feels settled. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes, doctor… or making sure I haven’t overlooked something. I don’t really need the surroundings to be a certain way. It’s just that before I can move on, I keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I’ve already checked them. And, you know, my wife is waiting and I feel badly about holding things up. I suppose I’m trying to get that feeling that it’s all right to leave it be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be symmetrical or exactly right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of things having to look even or line up exactly. It’s more… I’m checking whether I missed something, I suppose. If a box is a little untidy, that doesn’t bother me much by itself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking to make sure everything is under control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair… though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s like I need to feel that I haven’t let something slip by before I can move on. So I’ll check the same boxes, or the lists, or those drawers at the condo again, even though I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting, and I know she’d probably like me to just leave it be, but I have trouble doing that until it feels settled. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes, doctor… or making sure I haven’t overlooked something. I don’t really need the surroundings to be a certain way. It’s just that before I can move on, I keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I’ve already checked them. And, you know, my wife is waiting and I feel badly about holding things up. I suppose I’m trying to get that feeling that it’s all right to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things if someone you trusted had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that would settle it… and sometimes it helps a bit if my wife says she already looked. But honestly, I think I’d still feel a pull to check for myself, at least with the boxes or the lists or those drawers at the condo. Not because I don’t trust her— I do. It’s more that I don’t feel quite ready to move on until I’ve looked again myself, even though I know perfectly well it’s already been checked. I know that probably sounds a little unnecessary.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s a little embarrassing, but I’ve been having trouble just… moving on from checking things. My wife is usually waiting for me, and I know it’s frustrating for her. It’s not terrible, exactly, but it’s enough that I thought maybe I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in a dramatic way, no. It’s more that I just can’t seem to feel finished with it until I’ve checked again. The boxes, the lists, the drawers in the condo—I’ll know perfectly well I already looked, but I still go back over them before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ll get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not that I expect something terrible to happen, exactly… but I keep getting this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be symmetrical or exactly right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of things having to look even or line up exactly. It’s more… I’m checking whether I missed something, I suppose. If a box is a little untidy, that doesn’t bother me much by itself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking to make sure everything is under control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair… though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s like I need to feel that I haven’t let something slip by before I can move on. So I’ll check the same boxes, or the lists, or those drawers at the condo again, even though I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting, and I know she’d probably like me to just leave it be, but I have trouble doing that until it feels settled. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes, doctor… or making sure I haven’t overlooked something. I don’t really need the surroundings to be a certain way. It’s just that before I can move on, I keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I’ve already checked them. And, you know, my wife is waiting and I feel badly about holding things up. I suppose I’m trying to get that feeling that it’s all right to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things if someone you trusted had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that would settle it… and sometimes it helps a bit if my wife says she already looked. But honestly, I think I’d still feel a pull to check for myself, at least with the boxes or the lists or those drawers at the condo. Not because I don’t trust her— I do. It’s more that I don’t feel quite ready to move on until I’ve looked again myself, even though I know perfectly well it’s already been checked. I know that probably sounds a little unnecessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you had someone you completely trusted to double-check everything for you?\n\nPatient: I think it might help, yes… I don’t want to dismiss that. If my wife double-checked everything, I’d feel somewhat better. But I’m not sure it would stop me completely. What brought me in, really, is that I still find myself going back over the same boxes, lists, and the condo drawers before I can move on, even when I know perfectly well I already looked. And then my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be, and I feel a bit foolish, but I still have that urge to check again myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it be, and I do know it must look a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true,” but the thought sort of comes back around anyway, and then it’s hard to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not a strict order, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel I have to go back over the same things—the boxes, the lists, the drawers—until it feels acceptable to stop. I know I’ve already looked, which is the silly part, but I still end up checking again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting, so I do try not to make too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a very specific manner?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s very specific, exactly… not like I have a set ritual I have to follow. It’s more that I keep checking the same boxes, my lists, the drawers in the condo, over and over until I can sort of convince myself I’m done. Even when I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I do feel a bit foolish about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to you or someone else?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of… preventing harm, no. I don’t usually think, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more that I can’t quite let it go until I’ve looked again—at the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers—even though I know perfectly well I already checked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it be, and I feel a bit embarrassed, but it’s hard to move on until I’ve done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words or phrases in your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I don’t think I have words or phrases I repeat in my head. It’s more the checking that brought me in—the boxes, my lists, those drawers at the condo. I’ll look, and then I know I’ve looked, but I still feel I need to check again before I can move on. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, so I do try to keep it from getting too ridiculous.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I suppose I do feel calmer once I’ve checked. Not in a dramatic way, but enough that I can finally move on. That’s really what brought me in — I keep going back to the same boxes, or my lists, or the drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. I don’t picture some disaster, but I get this nagging thought that I might have tossed out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I’ll need in the wrong box. And I tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely, you already looked,” but the thought sort of comes back around and makes it hard to feel settled. I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of missing something important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair to say. And even when I tell myself, “No, that’s unlikely, you already checked,” it comes back again and I have trouble settling until I look. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd stop checking if you were absolutely certain you hadn't made a mistake?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back. It’s just… what brought me in is that I end up checking the same boxes, the lists, those drawers at the condo, over and over before I can move on. And I do know I already looked — I know that perfectly well — but it’s hard to leave it alone, especially with my wife waiting and probably thinking, “Come on, enough already.”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is in its right place, or about preventing mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about preventing a mistake, I think. I don’t mind things being in the right place, of course, but what seems to catch me is this feeling that I may have missed something. So I go back through the same boxes, the lists, sometimes the condo drawers, even though I know I already looked. It’s a little embarrassing, really, because my wife is waiting and I know she’d like me to just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need things to be symmetrical or exactly right when you're checking them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in the sense of things having to look even or line up exactly. It’s more… I’m checking whether I missed something, I suppose. If a box is a little untidy, that doesn’t bother me much by itself. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking to make sure everything is under control?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s fair… though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s like I need to feel that I haven’t let something slip by before I can move on. My wife will be waiting, and I know she’d probably like me to just leave it be, but I have trouble doing that until it feels settled. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes, doctor… or making sure I haven’t overlooked something. I don’t really need the surroundings to be a certain way. It’s just that before I can move on, I keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I’ve already checked them. And, you know, my wife is waiting and I feel badly about holding things up. I suppose I’m trying to get that feeling that it’s all right to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things if someone you trusted had already checked them for you?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, that would settle it… and sometimes it helps a bit if my wife says she already looked. But honestly, I think I’d still feel a pull to check for myself, at least with the boxes or the lists or those drawers at the condo. Not because I don’t trust her— I do. It’s more that I don’t feel quite ready to move on until I’ve looked again myself, even though I know perfectly well it’s already been checked. I know that probably sounds a little unnecessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you had someone you completely trusted to double-check everything for you?\n\nPatient: I think it might help, yes… I don’t want to dismiss that. If my wife double-checked everything, I’d feel somewhat better. But I’m not sure it would stop me completely. What brought me in, really, is that I still find myself going back over the same boxes, lists, and the condo drawers before I can move on, even when I know perfectly well I already looked. And then my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be, and I feel a bit foolish, but I still have that urge to check again myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding mistakes, doctor… if that makes sense. What brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, lists, and those condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know I already looked. My wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know it probably seems unnecessary, but checking again sort of settles me for the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably closer to it. If I could really feel certain that I hadn’t made a mistake, I probably wouldn’t keep going back.
